        Unconditional Integer Factorization in Time LN [1/3, O(1)]∗



                                                    Abstract
           We construct an unconditional, zero-error Las Vegas algorithm on a uniform classical multitape
        Turing machine that computes the complete prime factorization of every integer N ≥ 2 in worst-
        case expected time LN [1/3, 105] and worst-case deterministic workspace LN [1/3, 40], where

                                     Lx (α, c) := exp c(ln x)α (ln ln x)1−α
                                                     (︁                     )︁


        and T (x) ≤ Lx [α, c] means lim supx→∞ (ln max(1, T (x)))/((ln x)α (ln ln x)1−α ) ≤ c. Every prime
        factor in the output is certified deterministically by the Agrawal–Kayal–Saxena primality test,
        and the product of prime powers is checked directly against N . Every implied constant and
        finite threshold in the proof is effectively computable, and the argument relies on no unproven
        hypotheses.
            This establishes the number-field-sieve exponent 1/3 unconditionally with a single explicit
        finite constant, improving on the rigorous LN [1/2, 1] bound of Lenstra and Pomerance (1992)
        and matching the asymptotic exponent of the heuristic General Number Field Sieve. The
        proof combines four self-contained ingredients: a Siegel-zero-free single-integer hybrid smooth
        weight that cancels local Euler factors in arithmetic progressions; a truncated discrete Laplace
        sampler for mod-N polynomials that decouples evaluation fibers; smoothness-cancelling backward
        telescoping along a bounded-depth geometric descent ladder; and Stark–Chebotarev square
        certification paired with deterministic 3-adic Newton–Hensel square roots, transcript-symmetric
        Dixon fruitfulness, and strong-induction self-reduction of cofactors.


1       Introduction
1.1     Main result
How fast can a classical algorithm provably factor an arbitrary integer N ≥ 2 without unproven
number-theoretic hypotheses? Subexponential complexity is measured by the two-parameter scale

                   Lx (α, c) := exp c(ln x)α (ln ln x)1−α          (x > e, α ∈ [0, 1], c ∈ R),
                                    (︁                       )︁


where ln x denotes the natural logarithm. For a nonnegative function T (x), we write T (x) ≤ Lx [α, c]
as x → ∞ when
                                           ln max(1, T (x))
                                   lim sup                    ≤ c.
                                     x→∞ (ln x)α (ln ln x)1−α

   For more than three decades, a wide gap separated rigorous factoring bounds from heuristic
algorithms. Dixon’s random-squares method [7] gave the first rigorous subexponential bound
    ∗

as the base model. There was no human interaction other than the initial prompt for Cogentic to generate a full
proof. The Cogentic harness was built by Yang Cai, Vineet Gupta, Yanchen Jiang, Christopher Liaw, Aranyak Mehta,
Grigoris Velegkas, and Di Wang.

LN [1/2, 2 2]. In 1992, Lenstra and Pomerance [12] proved an unconditional zero-error Las Vegas
factoring bound of LN [1/2, 1] using random imaginary quadratic class groups. By contrast, the
General Number [︁Field Sieve (GNFS;   see Buhler, Lenstra, and Pomerance [3] and Coppersmith [5])
runs in time LN 1/3, (64/9)1/3 , but only under unproven smoothness, splitting, and full-rank
                                ]︁

heuristics.
    In this paper, we prove unconditionally that every integer N ≥ 2 can be completely factored
in worst-case expected time LN [1/3, 105] and worst-case deterministic workspace LN [1/3, 40]. Our
computational model is a single uniform classical probabilistic multitape Turing machine M
equipped with a read-once tape of independent fair coin flips ω ∈ {0, 1}N (defined formally in
Section 2.1). Let TM (N ; ω) denote the total number of bit operations before halting, and let
SM (N ) := supω∈{0,1}N SM (N ; ω) denote the worst-case deterministic workspace across all work,
input, and output tapes.
Theorem 1.1 (Unconditional Las Vegas factorization in time LN [1/3, 105]). There exists an
unconditional, zero-error Las Vegas uniform probabilistic multitape Turing machine M with the
following properties for every integer N ≥ 2 given in standard binary representation:
 (1) M(N ) halts with probability 1 over its internal fair coin flips ω ∈ {0, 1}N . Every halting
     execution outputs the exact complete prime factorization N = ki=1 pei i (p1 < · · · < pk prime,
                                                                    ∏︁

     ei ∈ N), certified deterministically via Agrawal–Kayal–Saxena primality testing of each pi and
     direct multiplication ki=1 pei i = N .
                           ∏︁

 (2) The worst-case expected number of bit operations satisfies
                                            ln Eω TM (N ; ω)
                                                   [︁          ]︁
                                    lim sup                        ≤ 105,
                                     N →∞ (ln N )
                                                 1/3 (ln ln N )2/3

      that is, Eω [TM (N ; ω)] ≤ LN [1/3, 105]. Moreover, the worst-case deterministic workspace
      satisfies SM (N ) ≤ LN [1/3, 40]. All implied constants and finite thresholds in the proof are
      effectively computable.
Remark 1.2 (Effectivity, uniformity, and worst-case workspace). Three features of Theorem 1.1
deserve emphasis:
 (a) Zero unproven hypotheses and effective constants. The proof uses only classical, peer-reviewed
     results of analytic and algebraic number theory, all with effective constants (Proposition 2.6).
     In particular, we do not invoke Siegel’s ineffective theorem on real zeros of Dirichlet L-functions.
     Every constant and threshold in our analysis is effectively computable.
 (b) Uniformity and finite-threshold safety. The machine M is a single uniform Turing machine
     with no advice strings and no hardcoded analytic constants. For small inputs, step-for-step
     interleaving with deterministic trial division guarantees zero-error termination on every coin
     tape ω.
 (c) Deterministic workspace. The workspace bound SM (N ) ≤ LN [1/3, 40] holds deterministically
     for every coin sequence ω ∈ {0, 1}N , not merely in expectation, because failed outer phases
     overwrite
         √      their scratch tapes and every recursive call operates on a strictly smaller integer
     ≤ 12 N . We have chosen transparent explicit constants (105 and 40) rather than optimizing
     them at the expense of clarity.

1.2   Overview of the proof for a general mathematician
To factor an odd composite integer N that is not a prime power, congruence-of-squares methods
seek two units Z, Y ∈ (Z/N Z)× such that Z 2 ≡ Y 2 (mod N ) and Z ̸≡ ±Y (mod N ). Then
gcd(Z − Y, N ) is a nontrivial divisor of N .

                                                   2
    In the General Number Field Sieve, one chooses an irreducible homogeneous polynomial
f (X, Y ) ∈ Z[X, Y ] with leading coefficient cd and degree d ≈ (ln N/ ln ln N )1/3 that vanishes
at a point (g0 , g1 ) (mod N ). Letting θ be an algebraic integer root of the associated monic polyno-
mial f˜, the assignment θ ↦→ cd g0 g1−1 mod N defines a ring homomorphism φ : Z[θ] → Z/N Z. One
then searches for coprime pairs (a, b) ∈ Z2 whose rational linear form ag1 − bg0 and algebraic norm
f (a, b) are both B-smooth (have all prime factors ≤ B), uses linear algebra over F2 to combine
relations into a simultaneous rational and algebraic square, and projects the two square roots into
Z/N Z.
    Four fundamental obstacles kept this paradigm heuristic for over three decades:
   (i) Siegel zeros in smooth-number arithmetic progressions. Proving that values along an arithmetic
       progression are y-smooth runs directly into possible Siegel zeros of Dirichlet L-functions modulo
       divisors of the step size.
  (ii) Polynomial-value smoothness across a descent. Current sieve methods cannot prove uncon-
       ditionally that a fixed high-degree polynomial f (a, b) takes B-smooth values at small points
       (a, b). Randomizing the polynomial solves one evaluation, but descending large primes across
       multiple levels introduces adaptive conditioning between parent and child evaluations.
 (iii) Algebraic square certification and square roots. Even prime-ideal valuations above unramified
       primes do not ensure that an algebraic number is a square, because of units, the 2-torsion
       of the class group, ramified primes, and the conductor of Z[θ] inside the maximal order OK .
       Moreover, computing the square root of a product of LN (1/3, c) algebraic numbers without
       height blowup requires a rigorous p-adic or Archimedean algorithm.
 (iv) Fruitfulness. Even after producing Z 2 ≡ Y 2 (mod N ), deterministic or correlated relations
       might yield the trivial congruence Z ≡ ±Y (mod N ).
   Our proof overcomes all four obstacles simultaneously through four interacting mechanisms.
Throughout this overview, write Λ1/3 := (ln N )1/3 (ln ln N )2/3 , Λ2/3 := (ln N )2/3 (ln ln N )1/3 , and
Υ := (ln N/ ln ln N )1/3 .

1. A Siegel-zero-free single-integer hybrid smooth weight (Section 3). Instead of counting
all y1 -smooth integers in a short arithmetic progression z0 + mAP t, we detect smooth integers with
a custom four-fold convolution weight w  ˜︁y1 ,u0 = (c0 ∗ c1 ∗ c1 ∗ c1 )/(4u0 )! (Definition 3.4). It counts
representations

2. Discrete Laplace polynomial sampling and mod-N decoupling (Section 4). To avoid
unproven smoothness heuristics for a fixed polynomial, we randomize two binary forms f1 , f2 of odd
degree d ≈ 12 Υ. We fix a base pair (g0 , g1 ) ∈ [G, 2G]2 with G ≈ LN (2/3, 3), pick a monic irreducible
h0 ∈ F3 [X] of degree d, and parameterize fc̃ (X, Y ) = di=0 (c̄i +3c̃i )X i Y d−i so that fc̃ (X, 1) ≡ h0 (X)

(mod 3) and fc̃ (g0 , g1 ) ≡ 0 (mod N ). Pinning fc̃ mod 3 = h0 automatically guarantees irreducibility
over Q and 3 ∤ fc̃ (a, b) for every primitive pair (a, b).
    Instead of a uniform box or a discrete Gaussian, we draw c̃(1) , c̃(2) independently from a truncated
discrete Laplace distribution GLap (c̃) = exp(−αLap ∥c̃∥1 )1c̃∈B˜︁ restricted to the mod-N coset L˜︁N
(Definition 4.1). Writing the ℓ1 exponential weight as a layer-cake mixture of axis-aligned boxes
[−Ri , Ri ] allows exact polynomial-time sampling via (3/4)-LLL lattice reduction (Lemma 4.2(2)).
Crucially, the Laplace weight gives both multiplicative shift invariance (Lemma 4.2(4)) and exact
decoupling of any R ≤ ⌈J/2⌉ + 3 polynomial evaluations from the mod-N constraint (Lemma 4.2(3)),
with tail error LN (1/3, −5) far smaller than the squared smoothness density LN (1/3, −3.88).

2      Preliminaries, Computational Model, and Parameter Schedule
This section establishes our notation, computational model, and standard Turing-machine primitives.
We then state four self-contained auxiliary propositions (whose proofs appear in Appendix A),
record the classical peer-reviewed theorems invoked in our proof, and specify our algorithmic and
analytic parameters in strict non-circular order.

2.1         Notation, computational model, and standard primitives
Throughout the paper, N = {1, 2, 3, . . . }; ln x denotes the natural logarithm for x > 0, and
log2 x := (ln x)/(ln 2). For a finite set S, |S| or #S denotes its cardinality. For n ∈ N, we write:
    • φ(n) := |(Z/nZ)× | for Euler’s totient function and µ(n) for the Möbius function;
    • ω(n) := p|n 1 for the number of distinct prime divisors of n and Ω(n) := pe ∥n e for the
               ∑︁                                                                  ∑︁

      number of prime divisors counted with multiplicity (with ω(1) := Ω(1) := 0);
    • π(x) := #{p prime : p ≤ x} and P + (n) := max({1} ∪ {p prime : p | n}), calling n ∈ Z \ {0}


                                                                 6
      y-smooth if P + (|n|) ≤ y.
Sums and products indexed by p, q, ℓ run over rational primes unless stated otherwise. For a prime
p and m ∈ Z \ {0}, vp (m) ∈ Z≥0 is the p-adic valuation (pvp (m) ∥ m). For (a, b) ∈ Z2 \ {(0, 0)}, we
write gcd(a, b) := gcd(|a|, |b|) ≥ 1 (with gcd(0, m) := |m|) and call (a, b) primitive if gcd(a, b) = 1.

Definition 2.1 (Uniform zero-error Las Vegas multitape Turing machine). A uniform zero-error
Las Vegas multitape Turing machine M is a single fixed multitape Turing machine equipped with a
read-once random-bit tape supplying independent fair coin flips ω = (ω1 , ω2 , . . . ) ∈ {0, 1}N at unit
cost per bit read, together with standard input, work, and output tapes. For each integer N ≥ 2
given in standard binary of bit-length b := ⌈log2 (N + 1)⌉, let TM (N ; ω) ∈ N ∪ {+∞} be the total
number of transitions before halting, and let SM (N ; ω) be the maximum number of tape cells visited
across the input, work, and output tapes (excluding the read-once coin tape, whose head never
moves left and stores no state). We require Prω [TM (N ; ω) < ∞] = 1 for every N ≥ 2, and that every
halting execution outputs the exact prime factorization N = ki=1 pei i with primes p1 < · · · < pk and
                                                              ∏︁

integers ei ≥ 1. The worst-case expected time is Eω [TM (N ; ω)], and the worst-case deterministic
workspace is SM (N ) := supω∈{0,1}N SM (N ; ω).

   We use the following standard primitives on a uniform multitape Turing machine:
   • Integer arithmetic. Addition, multiplication, division with remainder, and extended Eu-
     clidean gcd on n-bit integers run deterministically in O(n2 ) bit operations and O(n) space
     (with multiplication in O(n log n) bit operations by Harvey and van der Hoeven [8, Theo-
     rem 1.1]). Modular exponentiation ae mod m runs in O((log2 (e + 1))(log2 m)2 ) bit operations
     and O(log2 m) space.
   • Deterministic (3/4)-LLL lattice reduction. Given a basis (w1 , . . . , wn ) of a full-rank lattice
     Λ ⊂ Zn with maxj ∥wj ∥2 ≤ B0 (B0 ≥ 2), the LLL algorithm of Lenstra, Lenstra, and
     Lovász [11, Proposition 1.26] computes a (3/4)-LLL-reduced basis (b1 , . . . , bn )—whose Gram–
     Schmidt orthogonalization (b∗1 , . . . , b∗n ) satisfies |µj,m | ≤ 1/2 for 1 ≤ m < j ≤ n and
     ∥b∗j+1 ∥22 ≥ ( 34 − µ2j+1,j )∥b∗j ∥22 ≥ 12 ∥b∗j ∥22 —in deterministic O(n6 (log2 B0 )3 ) bit operations and
     O(n3 log2 B0 ) space.
   • Deterministic primality certification. By the Agrawal–Kayal–Saxena theorem [1, Theorem 5.1]
     and its errata [2], testing whether an integer n ≥ 2 is prime runs deterministically in

                                                          7
     O((log2 n)11 ) bit operations and O((log2 n)7 ) space with effective constants. Indeed, Lemma 4.3
     of [1] bounds the auxiliary modulus by r ≤ max{3, ⌈(log2 n)5 ⌉}, so polynomial residues and
     multiplications modulo (X r − 1, n) occupy at most O(r(log2 n) log log(n + 3)) ⊆ O((log2 n)7 )
     bits of workspace.
   • Deterministic perfect-power extraction. For n ≥ 2 and each integer k with 2 ≤ k ≤ ⌊log2 n⌋,
     binary search on [2, 2⌈(log2 n)/k⌉ ] checks whether n = mk for some m ∈ N. This takes total
     time O((log2 n)4 ) and space O(log2 n). Iterating writes n = nk00 with k0 ∈ N and n0 ≥ 2 not a
     perfect power.
   • Exact uniform and Bernoulli sampling. To sample uniformly on {0, 1, . . . , K − 1} (K ∈ N),
     return 0 if K = 1; otherwise set m := ⌈log2 K⌉, draw m fair bits as u ∈ {0, . . . , 2m − 1},
     and repeat until u < K. Each attempt succeeds with probability K/2m > 1/2, taking
     expected time O(log2 (K + 1)) and deterministic space O(log2 (K + 1)) by overwriting u.
     Capping at T attempts fails with probability < 2−T while keeping the conditional output law
     given acceptance equal to Unif({0, . . . , K − 1}). For integers 0 ≤ P ≤ Q (Q ≥ 1), drawing
     u ∼ Unif({0, . . . , Q−1}) and returning 1u<P realizes an exact Bernoulli(P/Q) coin in expected
     time and deterministic space O(log2 (Q + 1)).
   • Exact monic irreducible sampling over F3 . Given an odd integer d ≥ 3, draw h0 (X) =
     X d + d−1i=0 h̄i X (h̄i ∈ {0, 1, 2}) uniformly from the 3 monic degree-d polynomials in F3 [X].
                       i                                       d
            ∑︁
                                            d                              d/ℓ
     Test deterministically whether X 3 ≡ X (mod h0 ) and gcd(X 3 − X, h0 ) = 1 in F3 [X] for
     every prime divisor ℓ | d. These two conditions hold if and only if h0 is irreducible over F3 .
                                                                               3d
     By Möbius inversion, there are d1 e|d µ(e)3d/e ≥ d1 (3d − d · 3d/3 ) ≥ 2d    such irreducibles (since
                                          ∑︁

     d·3  −2d/3 ≤ 1/3 < 1/2 for d ≥ 3), so each proposal succeeds with probability ≥ 2d        1

     expected O(d ) bit operations and deterministic O(d ) space.

2.2    Auxiliary lattice, algebraic, and analytic tools
We begin with four self-contained auxiliary propositions on mod-N polynomial lattices, two-
dimensional lattice reduction, discrete homogeneous linear forms, and monogenic orders. To keep
the main narrative focused, we defer their elementary proofs to Appendix A.

2.3   Non-circular parameter schedule
Every algorithmic quantity below is computed deterministically from N in strict dependency order,
with no circular references and no hidden analytic constants.

Lemma 2.8 (Computability and asymptotic parameter inequalities). For every integer N > Nalg ,
all algorithmic quantities in Definition 2.7 (evaluating Eq , eH0 (p), and (y∗ (X∗ , Yk ), u0 (X∗ , Yk ))
on demand, testing ParamsValid(N ), and representing xch = 2Kch by Kch ) are deterministically
computable in (ln N )O(1) bit operations and (ln N )O(1) space. Moreover, there exists an effective

3     Siegel-Free Short-Interval Hybrid Smoothness and Lattice Ge-
      ometry
This section builds the analytic and lattice tools that replace heuristic smoothness assumptions. We
first construct Gevrey-2 logarithmic cutoffs, an explicit hybrid large sieve, and a truncated Brun sieve
on short intervals (Lemmas 3.1–3.3). We then introduce the four-fold hybrid smooth weight w       ˜︁y1 ,u0
and prove its unconditional, Siegel-zero-free asymptotic in short arithmetic progressions (Lemma 3.5).
Finally, we prove the special-q lattice-box counting, skewness excision, and bad-conductor avoidance
estimates needed for every descent level (Lemma 3.6).

    We now introduce our four-fold hybrid smooth weight. Because it contains one unrestricted
dyadic integer m1 alongside 4u0 − 1 dyadic primes, small common factors g0 = gcd(z0 , mAP ) are
forced into m1 , cancelling local Euler factors and eliminating all Siegel-zero obstructions.

Lemma 3.6 (Lattice-box sampling, skewness excision, and bad-conductor geometry). Let N ≥ N0
have no prime factors ≤ B, and fix g0 , g1 ∈ [G, 2G] ∩ Z with gcd(g0 , g1 ) = gcd(g0 g1 , N ) = 1. Define

 (1) Exceptional set sizes. We have |Sbad | ≤ LN (1/3, 1.48) and |Qexc | ≤ LN (1/3, 1.49). Every

 (2) Non-skewed special-q box count and exact sampler. For any prime q ∈ (B, Q0 ] and rq ∈

4     Discrete Laplace Polynomial Coupling, Concentration, and Path
      Telescoping
This section builds the randomized two-field polynomial ensemble and proves that a single random
pair (f1 , f2 ) supports full prime-ideal descent across the geometric ladder Y0 > Y1 > · · · > YJ = B.
We first define the mod-3-pinned discrete Laplace law and establish its exact layer-cake LLL sampler,
mod-N evaluation decoupling, and multiplicative shift invariance (Lemma 4.2). We next prove
Level-0 rational smoothing (Lemma 4.3) and local second-moment concentration at every descent
depth (Lemma 4.4). Finally, we specify the bounded-budget subroutine DescentTree (Definition 4.5)
and propagate local concentration globally by smoothness-cancelling backward path telescoping
(Lemma 4.6).

                                                            40
5     Algebraic Square Certification, 3-Adic Newton Roots, and Fruit-
      fulness
This section supplies the three algebraic pillars that convert descended relations into a nontrivial
congruence of squares modulo N . First, Lemma 5.1 bounds the enlarged square-obstruction group
VA′ ι /(Kι× )2 and eliminates all but a 2-dimensional kernel using random degree-1 quadratic characters
certified by Stark’s lemma, Landau–Page repulsion, and the effective Chebotarev density theorem.
Second, Lemma 5.2 extracts exact algebraic square roots deterministically via quadratic 3-adic
Newton–Hensel lifting in the unramified extension Z3 [θι ]. Third, Lemma 5.3 proves that hiding each
secret root zm until after Y is computed yields an exact splitting probability of 1 − 21−ω(N ) ≥ 1/2.

Lemma 5.1 (Enlarged obstruction group and Stark–Chebotarev square certification). Let N ≥ N0 .
For ι ∈ {1, 2}, let fι = fc̃(ι) satisfy Lemma 4.2(1) with gcd(cι,d , N ) = 1, and let Kι , θι , Aι , f˜ι , γι (a, b)
be as in Section 4, with ∆ ˜ ι := disc(f˜ι ) ̸= 0.
  (1) Local length away from cι,d . For every primitive pair (a, b) ∈ Z2 and every prime p ∤ cι,d : we
      have NKι /Q (γι (a, b)) = cd−1
                                 ι,d fι (a, b); if p ∤ fι (a, b), then lp,Aι (γι (a, b)) = 0 for all p ∈ Max(Aι )

      maximal ideal p = (p, θι − (cι,d ab−1 mod p)) of Aι , and lp′ ,Aι (γι (a, b)) = 0 at every other
      maximal ideal p′ ∋ p of Aι .
  (2) Enlarged obstruction group VA′ ι . Define

6    Complete Algorithm and Proof of Theorem 1.1
We now assemble all components into a single uniform zero-error Las Vegas multitape Turing
machine M (Definition 6.1) and prove Theorem 1.1 in four steps: zero-error finite termination on
all N ≥ 2, worst-case deterministic workspace LN [1/3, 40], constant outer-phase success probability
> 1/3, and strong-induction expected runtime LN [1/3, 105].

Definition 6.1 (The uniform Las Vegas factoring machine M). Define two mutually recursive
zero-error procedures on a uniform probabilistic multitape Turing machine: Factor(N ) (which takes
any integer N ≥ 2 and returns its sorted certified prime-power factorization) and SingleSplit(N )
(which takes any odd composite non-prime-power integer N > Nalg free of prime factors ≤ B and
returns a certified divisor 1 < d∗ < N ).
Procedure Factor(N ):
  (1) If N ≤ Nalg = 210000 , factor N completely by deterministic trial division up to N , certify each
      prime factor by AKS, check that the product equals N , and return.
  (2) Strip off 2v2 (N ) . Initialize a worklist W := {N/2v2 (N ) } (if > 1) and a multiset O of certified
      primes holding v2 (N ) copies of 2. While W ̸= ∅, pop an element N1 ∈ W:


                                                    44
        • Extract N1 = N2k2 deterministically (Section 2.1; k2 ∈ N, N2 ≥ 3 not a perfect power).
          Test N2 by AKS; if prime, insert k2 copies of N2 into O and continue.
        • If N2 ≤ Nalg , set d∗ := min{t ≥ 3 : t | N2 }. Otherwise compute B = B(N2 )
          (Definition 2.7) and trial-divide N2 by all odd 3 ≤ t ≤ min(B, N2 − 2). If a divisor
          1 < d∗ < N2 is found, push k2 copies of d∗ and k2 copies of N2 /d∗ onto W and continue.
        • Otherwise invoke d∗ ← SingleSplit(N2 ), verify deterministically that 1 < d∗ < N2 and
          d∗ | N2 , and push k2 copies of d∗ and k2 copies of N2 /d∗ onto W.
 (3) Sort and group O as pe11 · · · pekk , verify AKS primality of each pi and exact multiplication
            ei
       i=1 pi = N , and return.
     ∏︁k


Procedure SingleSplit(N ): Compute the parameters of Definition 2.7 and test ParamsValid(N ).
If ParamsValid(N ) is false, find and return min{t ≥ 3 : t | N } by deterministic trial division.
Otherwise, run the randomized outer-phase loop below interleaved step-for-step (1 transition for 1
transition) with a deterministic background thread performing trial division of N by odd integers
3, 5, 7, . . . , N − 2. Whichever thread first finds d∗ ∈ (1, N ) with d∗ | N halts both threads and
returns d∗ . Each iteration of the outer-phase loop overwrites its phase workspace (capping every
elementary uniform-integer draw at ⌈10(L   ˆ︁ 1 + 1)2 ⌉ attempts and restarting on timeout) and executes
five steps:
Step 1. Base pair and polynomial setup. Draw (g0 , g1 ) ∼ Unif(Gbox ) (Lemma 4.3; capped at
        ⌈103 (L  ˆ︁ 1 +1)2 ⌉ proposals), draw a monic irreducible h0 ∈ F3 [X] of odd degree d (Section 2.1;
        capped at ⌈20d(L       ˆ︁ 1 + 1)2 ⌉ proposals), and draw c̃(1) , c̃(2) independently from Palg via
        Lemma 4.2(2) (capping each LLL-box rejection loop at ⌈10(L            ˆ︁ 1 + 1)2 ⌉ proposals), restarting
        the phase on any timeout. Set fι := fc̃(ι) (ι = 1, 2). For ι ∈ {1, 2}, compute g∗ :=
        gcd(cι,d , N ) and g∗′ := gcd(g1d−1 f˜ι′ (cι,d mN ), N ); if either lies in (1, N ), return it, and if
        either equals N , restart the phase.
Step 2. Factor base and quadratic characters. Enumerate all primes p ≤ B by trial division. For
        each ι ∈ {1, 2} and each prime p ≤ B with p ∤ cι,d , enumerate all roots s ∈ {0, . . . , p − 1} of
        f˜ι (s) ≡ 0 (mod p) to build the degree-1 factor base Fι (B) (|Fι (B)| ≤ dπ(B)). Sample 2Lch
        character pairs (ℓι,j , sι,j ) ∈ Ich (ι) (ι ∈ {1, 2}, 1 ≤ j ≤ Lch ) via Lemma 5.1(3) (capped at
        ⌈exp(2Λ     ˆ︁ )⌉ proposals and ⌈20d(L
                      1/3
                                                 ˆ︁ 1 + 1)2 ⌉ Cantor–Zassenhaus splits; restart on timeout).
        Set Drow := 3 + π(B) + |F1 (B)| + |F2 (B)| + 2Lch and Rtot := Drow + 3.

               wm := zm  2 mod N .

Proof of Proposition 2.3. Part (1). Order the input basis so ∥w1 ∥2 ≤ ∥w2 ∥2 , and repeat: set
k := ⌊⟨w1 , w2 ⟩/∥w1 ∥22 + 12 ⌋ ∈ Z and w2′ := w2 − kw1 , so |⟨w1 , w2′ ⟩| ≤ 12 ∥w1 ∥22 . If ∥w2′ ∥2 ≥ ∥w1 ∥2 , halt
and return (w1 , w2′ ); otherwise swap (w1 , w2 ) ← (w2′ , w1 ) and continue.
     Suppose a non-halting step satisfies ∥w2′ ∥22 > 31 ∥w1 ∥22 . In the immediately following iteration,
|⟨w2′ , w1 ⟩| ≤ 12 ∥w1 ∥22 < 23 ∥w2′ ∥22 , so the next rounding integer k ′ lies in {−1, 0, 1}. If k ′ = 0, then
∥w1 ∥2 > ∥w2′ ∥2 halts immediately; if k ′ = ±1, then w1′ := w1 − k ′ w2′ obeys ∥w1′ ∥22 = ∥w1 ∥22 −
2|⟨w1 , w2′ ⟩| + ∥w2′ ∥22 ≥ ∥w2′ ∥22 (since 2|⟨w1 , w2′ ⟩| ≤ ∥w1 ∥22 ), which also halts immediately. Hence every
non-final swap shrinks ∥w2′ ∥22 ≤ 13 ∥w1 ∥22 , capping the iteration count at 2 + log3 (B02 ). All vectors
remain bounded in norm by B0 , so each step takes O((ln B0 )2 ) bit operations and O(ln B0 ) space,
giving total time O((ln B0 )3 ) and space O(ln B0 ).
     For any reduced basis (v1 , v2(︁), we have |⟨v1 , v2 ⟩|        ≤ 12 ∥v1 ∥22 ≤ 12 ∥v1 ∥2 ∥v2 ∥2 . By the AM–GM
inequality, |x1 x2 | ∥v1 ∥2 ∥v2 ∥2 ≤ 2 x21 ∥v1 ∥22 + x22 ∥v2 ∥22 , and therefore
                                          1
                                                                )︁


                                                                                     1 (︁ 2
         ∥x1 v1 + x2 v2 ∥22 ≥ x21 ∥v1 ∥22 + x22 ∥v2 ∥22 − |x1 x2 | ∥v1 ∥2 ∥v2 ∥2 ≥       x1 ∥v1 ∥22 + x22 ∥v2 ∥22 .
                                                                                                                 )︁
                                                                                     2
Now take any integers x1 , x2 with x2 ̸= 0:
   • if x1 = 0, then ∥x2 v2 ∥2 ≥ ∥v2 ∥2 ;
   • if |x1 | ≥ |x2 | ≥ 1, then 2|⟨v1 , v2 ⟩| ≤ ∥v1 ∥22 yields ∥x1 v1 + x2 v2 ∥22 ≥ x22 ∥v2 ∥22 + |x1 |(|x1 | −
     |x2 |)∥v1 ∥22 ≥ ∥v2 ∥22 ;
   • if |x2 | > |x1 | ≥ 1, then |x1 x2 | − x21 > 0 and ∥v1 ∥22 ≤ ∥v2 ∥22 yield ∥x1 v1 + x2 v2 ∥22 ≥ x22 ∥v2 ∥22 −
     (|x1 x2 | − x21 )∥v1 ∥22 ≥ (x22 − |x1 x2 | + x21 )∥v2 ∥22 ≥ ∥v2 ∥22 .
Thus every vector in Λ \ Zv1 has norm ≥ ∥v2 ∥2 , while every nonzero vector in Zv1 has norm
≥ ∥v1 ∥2 . This proves ∥v1 ∥2 = λ1 (Λ) and ∥v2 ∥2 = λ2 (Λ). Finally, det(Λ)2 = ∥v1 ∥22 ∥v2 ∥22 − ⟨v1 , v2 ⟩2 ∈
[ 43 ∥v1 ∥22 ∥v2 ∥22 , ∥v1 ∥22 ∥v2 ∥22 ].

valid pairs (A, B), put u := p∈A p and v := q∈B q. Then gcd(u, v) = 1, 1 ≤ u, v ≤ Qr00 ≤ 21 N ,
                                 ∏︁                ∏︁

and zA,B := uv −1 mod N ∈ QN . The map (A, B) ↦→ zA,B is injective: if u1 v1−1 ≡ u2 v2−1 (mod N ),
then N | (u1 v2 − u2 v1 ) with |u1 v2 − u2 v1 | < 41 N , forcing u1 v2 = u2 v1 , hence (u1 , v1 ) = (u2 , v2 )
and (A1 , B1 ) = (A2 , B2 ) by coprimality and √︁    unique prime factorization. Moreover, whenever
z = zA,B , (u, v) ∈ Lz \ √︁{(0, 0)} has ∥(u, v)∥2 ≤ N/2. Any shortest nonzero vector (x1 , y1 ) of Lz
satisfies ∥(x1 , y1 )∥2 ≤ N/2 and x1 v − y1 u ≡ 0 (mod N ). By Cauchy–Schwarz, |x1 v − y1 u| ≤
∥(x1 , y1 )∥2 ∥(v, −u)∥2 ≤ N/2 < N , forcing x1 v = y1 u. Since gcd(u, v) = 1 and ∥(x1 , y1 )∥2 ≤
∥(u, v)∥2 , normalizing x1 > 0 gives (x1 , y1 ) = (u, v). Dividing N (Q, r0 ) by |QN | and using |GN /QN |·
|QN | = φ(N ) finishes the proof.

Proof of Proposition 2.5. Part (1). Because A = Z[θ] is a 1-dimensional Noetherian domain, for
each p ∈ Max(A) and x, y ∈ A \ {0}, the quotient Ap /xAp has finite length, and multiplication
by x gives the short exact sequence 0 → Ap /yAp → Ap /xyAp → Ap /xAp → 0. Thus lp,A (xy) =
lp,A (x) + lp,A (y), which extends uniquely to K × = Frac(A)× via lp,A (x/y) := lp,A (x) − lp,A (y). Since
 ˜ = [OK : A]2 |∆K |, every prime p ∤ ∆

OK,P is a discrete valuation ring whose Jordan–Hölder length equals ordP .
